ParlaMint-SI_2019-06-18-SDZ8-Redna-09.ana.u134	Dear Presidents, honourable Members, a nice welcome from me and also from the Secretary of State of the Ministry of Defence. We have had this initiative several times in the past when we are supposed to transfer certain powers conferred by the Government of the Republic of Slovenia when it comes to fulfilling certain obligations as a member of international organisations. Here, in particular, I stress that Slovenia is a member of the United Nations, the European Union and NATO and that as a member we also fulfil certain obligations. Then there are also certain international treaties which also impose certain obligations that Slovenia must carry out and implement on the basis of these commitments. This is also the case for deployment of members of the Slovenian Army to international operations and missions. And such attempts to change this relationship between the Government and the National Assembly have in the past been quite something, in 2006, 2010 and 2016. But these experiments were not successful. The National Assembly has not decided to change the relationship between the Government and the National Assembly when it comes to meeting the international obligations that Slovenia has as a member of this organisation and even when it comes to fulfilling the commitments we have under international treaties. However, the Strategy for the participation of the Republic of Slovenia in international operations and missions was adopted in 2010, which strengthened the role of the National Assembly when it comes to deployments to international operations and missions. The National Assembly should give an opinion after a prior consultation, which means that the Government first accepts the intention to participate in a particular international operation or mission, then goes to the National Assembly to the competent committees, namely the Foreign Policy Committee and the Defence Committee. And then, on the basis of this opinion, the Government takes the final decision. I must say that so far these opinions have always been positive, usually the government has also taken a final decision to participate in international operations and missions. In doing so, I would like to point out that the mandate of an international organisation is always at the back of all the missions involving the Slovenian Armed Forces. Either it is the United Nations Security Council or it is a decision at European Union level. The government does not agree that we would partially resolve this issue in terms of opening the Defence Act only on this subject, but we want to address this area when we open the Defence Act, which requires 60 votes from Members of the National Assembly, where we will also address some other issues that are important for the further development of the Slovenian Army. Here, in particular, I am referring to some of the status issues that are not adequately addressed. It is necessary to know that the Defence Act dates back to the 1990s and has been a long time and that things have also changed. Since the Defence Committee has adopted a position to hold a general debate on the Resolution on the Strategy for National Security, our proposal is that this debate on the regulation of the relations between the Government and the National Assembly, when it comes to the deployment of our members of the Slovenian Armed Forces to an international operation and mission, be held in this forum. There will be experts, politicians and I think it would be appropriate to hold this debate earlier. As I know, this proposal was also supported by the Left, which called for a general debate when it comes to a resolution on a national security strategy. For the time being, we believe that we are going to go into a procedure that is broad-based, as far as national security is concerned, because we know that we are going to reform all these strategic documents, such as the national security strategy, the Defence Act, the medium-term defence programme, including new threats such as hybrid threats, cyber threats. The government opposes that at this point we should change this law on defence; however, we want this debate to take place within the framework of the Resolution on the Strategy for National Security.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-06-18-SDZ8-Redna-09.ana.u135	Thank you very much. We are moving on to a presentation on the views of the groups of Members. Member Jožef Horvat has the floor to present the position of the New Slovenia Group – Christian Democrats. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-06-18-SDZ8-Redna-09.ana.u136	Thank you very much. Mr Vice-President of the National Assembly, Minister-in-Charge, Honourable Applicant, Fellow Members and Fellow Members! The objective of the draft Law on the Complementation of the Defence Act, proposed by the Parliamentary Group of the Left, is to ensure the transparency and democratic nature of the decision to send Slovenian Armed Forces units to military operations abroad or to integrate them into the multinational units and activities of NATO. If we look closely at the content, this is a supplement to Article 82 of the Defence Act, which lays down the powers of the National Defence Council. The new paragraphs 5 and 6 of paragraph 1 are added which provide, for the first time, that the Government must obtain the consent of the National Assembly to acts setting out the type, scope and other rules of multi-annual guidance for the participation of the State, in particular the Slovenian Armed Forces and other defence and civilian capacities in international operations and missions. The National Assembly would therefore agree on the general planning acts in this area. The other important content of the law is that the Government must obtain prior agreement from the National Assembly on the proposals for the deployment of units of the Slovenian Armed Forces to a new international operation or early termination or change of purpose of the operation; or for the deployment of units to carry out the preparation and tasks of the joint defence within the framework of NATO. Consent should only be required when referring to new operations or the suspension or modification of the purpose of the operation and the formation of multinational units, but not in the event of rotations and changes in contingents in existing operations or the appointment of representatives to international organisations. I must make it very clear that the Section for Defence of New Slovenia has examined in detail the proposal for the Law on Complements of the Defence Act and adopted a negative position. This position is also followed by the position of the New Slovenia Group. We are not going to support the draft law on the amendments to the Defence Act, as proposed by the Left; however, we are not going to support the experience of such, always very careful when it comes to proposals on defence by the Left Group. We must not forget that on the Committee on Foreign Policy on 17 August last year, the Parliamentary Group of the Left, on the day of the State holiday, when the National Assembly later elected the President of the Government, blocked the purchase of equipment needed for the Slovenian Army. Later the government had to swallow it somehow, she had to accept it. ParlaMint-SI_2019-06-18-SDZ8-Redna-09.ana.u138	Thank you very much, Chairman. Dear Minister, Secretary of State, fellow Members and Members of Parliament! Today we have before us a bill that is more or less identical to the proposal of the previous Assembly. At that time, after the discussion on the working body, there was insufficient support for further consideration and similar proposals were made in 2006 and 2010. In the proposal for a law, it is once again an attempt to supplement Articles 82 and 84 of the Defence Act, which provides that the powers of the National Assembly and the Government in the management and control of the defence of the State are to be exercised. The proposed amendments relate to the procedure for seconding members of the Slovenian Armed Forces to international operations and missions. More specifically, proposers at the legal level want to provide that the Government must obtain the consent of the National Assembly prior to the deployment of the Slovenian Armed Forces units to international missions or operations. The draft law refers, inter alia, to the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia, which provides in article 92 for the use of defence forces to be decided by the National Assembly. It is interesting, however, that they have forgotten to mention the fact that this article relates only to war and state of emergency. It is true that the coalition partners are committed to finding a solution to this mandate, as proposed by the proposaler. However, in the Alenka Bratušek Group, we believe that a constructive solution must be found for such questions, which will still allow sufficient efficiency; and therefore a careful and sound consideration is needed. We will not support today's proposal in the Parliamentary Group of the Alenka Bratusek Party. We agree with the Government's view that such complex issues require an in-depth approach. The expert debate can thus be held in the context of a public presentation of opinions, which will take place on the subject of the Resolution on the Strategy for National Security. Although the power to decide on the deployment of Slovenian soldiers abroad is not legally established, the National Assembly is already involved in the decision-making process. In 2010, the Government adopted the Strategy for the participation of the Republic of Slovenia in international operations and missions, which provides for a more detailed procedure for the deployment of members of the Slovenian Armed Forces abroad. This strategy lays down a two-phase decision-making procedure in Article 39, which means that the Government shall send each such initiative to the National Assembly before the final decision is taken. According to the Ministry of Defence, since 2009, no decision has been taken by the Government in this area without a prior discussion in the National Assembly. When deciding to send members of the Slovenian Armed Forces abroad, response and effectiveness are essential, which could be extremely difficult due to the proposed amendments which provide for consensus in the National Assembly. In doing so, I stress that the Government is already taking into account the guidelines adopted by the National Assembly when making such decisions. Once again, in the Parliamentary Group of the Party, Alenka Bratušek, we assess that the draft Law on Complements of the Defence Act is not suitable for further consideration and we will not support it. However, we are prepared to participate in further discussions in finding solutions for any changes in this area. In doing so, we will advocate the preservation of democratic principles and respect for the principle of the division of power. Let me end by adding that the security situation around the world is very unpredictable. At any time, there may be a situation where quick but difficult decisions need to be made to send Slovenian soldiers abroad, especially to crisis hotspots. Such decisions should be made on a serious basis, as otherwise they may lead to serious and serious consequences. Thank you very much.

Let me remind you that the Strategy for the participation of the Republic of Slovenia in international operations and missions has been in place for ten years. This act introduced a two-phase procedure so that the initiative for the participation of the Republic of Slovenia in military operations or civilian missions is first sent to the National Assembly and then to the Government. When considering other circumstances, it shall take its final decision. The strategy also provides that the National Assembly should be informed once a year of its participation in ongoing international operations and missions, as well as of the implementation of the strategy itself. Today it is the focus of the Slovenian Army's activities in international operations and missions in Kosovo and Afghanistan. Its members are also present in Bosnia and Herzegovina, Lebanon, Syria, Somalia, Serbia and Macedonia. The Slovenian army is already active in the European Union's fighting group and NATO's response forces, which are intended to operate on operations within and outside the borders of the Alliance. According to available data, currently 348 members and members of the Slovenian Armed Forces are present on 12 international missions and operations, of which there are a maximum of 236, in Kosovo. In the Desus Group, we are aware that, in particular, we have recently witnessed rapid changes in the security environment and that international missions are also becoming increasingly difficult. Therefore, in our parliamentary group today, we will not support the conclusion that the law is suitable for further consideration.

Following Slovenia's entry into the European Union and NATO 15 years ago, which was also a key objective in the field of national defence policy, the international situation of our country has changed significantly. In particular, the security environment has changed, which is not a static factor and needs to be continuously adapted. The development of the capacity of the Slovenian Armed Forces has thus taken place in the direction of achieving these objectives. The challenge was aimed at creating transferable capacities, which also allowed for the envisaged scope of action in NATO’s response forces, the European Union’s combat teams and crisis response operations. The process of integration into the structure of NATO was intensive and, in the development of the Slovenian Armed Forces, there were gradually delays in the establishment of planned capacities and an ever-increasing gap between the real capacities and commitments undertaken in the Alliance. This gap has increased further as the economic and financial crisis spreads. The deployment of the professional army and the single payroll system and some other solutions in the past period have increasingly highlighted the need to complement the fundamental strategic, developmental and normative solutions in the defence field. In 2009, a strategic review of the Defence Resource was therefore prepared to review the scope and structure of the capacity of this Resource and its functioning and management, in particular in terms of efficiency and rationality, and to contribute to the credibility of the Republic of Slovenia in the international community. On the basis of this review, any errors and shortcomings should be addressed and a short- and long-term vision of development based on the real staffing, material and other capacities of the country should be ensured. In particular, it follows from the findings of this review that the development, equipment and functioning of the Slovenian Armed Forces must be based on the balance of ambitions, needs and capabilities of the State as a Member State of NATO and of the European Union. That the greatest attention must be paid to ensuring the key capacities arising from NATO's commitments and to effectively complement the forces for international operations and missions. As in all democratic societies, there are different views on this issue in the Republic of Slovenia. Decisions to participate in all these guidelines have been taken behind closed doors, the country costs millions and millions of euros, and their contribution to international peace and security is often questionable and is even negative, as is the case with missions in Iraq, Afghanistan and Latvia mentioned earlier. ParlaMint-SI_2019-06-18-SDZ8-Redna-09.ana.u253	Thank you for your word, Mr. President. Dear Members and Members! The current members of the Board of the Agency, with the exception of the Director as President of the Board of the Agency, shall expire on 14 July this year, which the Government informed the National Assembly in March. The Government then proceeded to implement the further legal obligations necessary for the appointment of members of the Board of the Agency, by issuing a public call to collect proposals for possible candidates in the Official Journal and by selecting the proposed candidates which the Government confirmed and submitted for approval to the National Assembly. The Law on the Market in Financial Instruments provides that the authorities of the Securities Market Agency shall be the Director and the Board of the Agency. The Council shall consist of 5 members, of whom the Director of the Agency shall also be the President of the Board of the Agency. Following the expiry of the public call, an independent commission was appointed at the Ministry for the selection of candidates for members of the Board of the Agency, which established criteria for the evaluation and selection of candidates. In doing so, the Commission considered that it was best to use a combination of the following criteria: to take account of the Agency’s Board’s interconnectivity with other regulators, in particular with the representatives of the Insurance Supervisory Agency and the Audit Authority, as the supervisory authorities also cooperate with each other in specific supervisory procedures. Given the complexity and nature of the tasks of the Council of the Agency, it makes sense that the Council consists of both external experts and at least one staff member of the Agency. It also makes sense to involve external experts who are not burdened by the Agency's internal activities. For the functioning of the Board of the Agency, it is important that the Council consists of experts who, with in-depth expertise in specific areas, can provide an independent view of the functioning of the securities market and the functioning of the Agency. On the basis of these baselines, the independent Commission has proposed to the Government that the Council of the Agency appoint Mag for a term of six years. It's Pinozo, Dr. Dragan Kesic, mag. Mount Chibei and Mag. Mojco Majich. The government believes that the proposed candidates will ensure the Agency's independent and professional functioning and contribute to the faster development of the capital market in Slovenia. At the same time, candidates will adequately support and ensure the effective functioning of capital markets at European Union level. Thank you very much.
